Emerging Technologies in IoM: 
AI, Blockchain and Beyond
Sumit Bansal1* and Vandana Sindhi2
1Department of Computer Science and Engineering, Gurukul Kangri (DU), 
Haridwar, Uttarakhand, India
2Department of Computer Science, Punjabi University, Patiala, Punjab, India
Abstract
In this chapter, the role of AI, blockchain, and emerging requirements will be 
analyzed as drivers of the revolutionary impact of emerging technologies for the 
Internet of Medicine (IoM). The chapter addresses the issue of the integration of 
these new technologies into the healthcare system, while at the same time trying to 
assess whether or not they are evidently efficient. This chapter offers various per­
spectives on how AI could transform healthcare administration, therapy person­
alization and diagnostics. Additionally, the essay investigates how the blockchain 
ledger can be innovatively exploited to protect medical data, maintain transpar­
ency and become a part of decentralized system of healthcare. The chapter studies 
other technologies that govern the Internet of Medical Management (IoMM) along 
with some enabling work of AI and Blockchain. It looks at their joint possibility 
as well as the moral issues emerging when they are used repeatedly. Researchers, 
practitioners, and policymakers aiming for a better understanding of how rapidly 
evolving technologies are transforming the healthcare sector may find this chap­
ter to be a main source of information as the healthcare industry goes through a 
massive change.
Keywords:  Internet of medicine (IoM), emerging technologies, artificial 
intelligence (AI), blockchain in healthcare, healthcare transformation
*Corresponding author: sumitbansal.cse@gkv.ac.in

306  Multimodal Data Fusion for Bioinformatics AI
14.1	 Introduction
The Internet of Medicine (IoM) with its seamless use of digital technology 
along with the old conventional medical layout has become a force of dis­
ruption that has positive impacts in treatment and diagnosis [1]. AI and 
blockchain demonstrate the leading role in this process; these slabs create 
a perfect and secure foundation for the IoM ecosystem [2, 3].
Already, healthcare has greatly benefited from artificial intelligence 
thanks to the technology’s ability to precisely diagnose medical condi­
tions, customize treatment regimens, and foresee certain health outcomes. 
Machine learning algorithms that are part of artificial intelligence have 
demonstrated the ability to realise the entirety of crucial actions such as 
pattern-detecting, the course of diseases forecasting and better treatment 
methods like treatment plan improvement [4]. The enormous potential 
that AI has in processing and analyzing a broad range of medical data at a 
faster pace, AI is highly positioned to advance the quality of care and boost 
more effective and patient-centred healthcare systems [5].
However, it is important to note that the safety and integrity of confiden­
tial patient data should be the number one priority since IoMM relies on 
the exchange of this data among different networks. Known for the features 
of impenetrability and decentralization, the blockchain technology is the 
solution to many interoperabilityand data security problems. Blockchain 
restricts the possibility of data breaches and brings secure data sharing 
among authorised institutions, and this digital ledger makes the records 
more reliable by being immutable and transparent [6].
Blockchain and AI are two technologies that work better in unison than 
separately, so to speak. The role of blockchain in giving secure transactions 
along with data integrity perfectly matches the needs of AI in terms of hav­
ing dependable dataset [7]. This partnership protects patient privacy and 
data authenticity while improving the accuracy of AI models and foster­
ing a sense of confidence within the IoM ecosystem. Furthermore, because 
blockchain is decentralised, there is less chance of a single point of fail­
ure, which keeps Internet of Medical Things (IoMT) systems resilient and 
operational.
14.1.1	
Importance of the Internet of Medicine
With respect to the IoM, AI integrated with blockchain is not only a 
technological trend; rather it is a model change with deep repercus­
sions for all occupants of healthcare [8]. This chapter is geared towards 

Emerging Technologies in IoM: AI, Blockchain and Beyond  307
demystifying this convergence and unpacking the potential of this inte­
gration to improve patient care, streamline administrative processes and 
facilitate joint research collaborations [9]. On our way through the realms 
of AI, blockchain, and beyond, the IoMT implications are enormous, turn­
ing the health care system into an intelligent, secured, and unprecedented 
innovatory sector full of revolutionary solutions.
14.2	 Artificial Intelligence (AI) in Healthcare
AI in healthcare encompasses the utilization of sophisticated computa­
tional algorithms, machine learning and big data analysis tools to acceler­
ate the discovery of new medicines, improve diagnosis accuracy, facilitate 
personalized treatment and streamline healthcare operations [10]. The 
introduction of AI into healthcare can revolutionize the entire industry, 
because it can help enhance productivity, decrease costs and improve 
patient outcomes. Figure 14.1 depicts where AI is likely to best comple­
ment different healthcare sectors.
14.2.1	
Diagnostic Imaging and Radiology
The high precision with which AI systems diagnose medical images of CT, 
MRI, and X-ray scans is achieved by the AI systems. By providing a more 
detailed and accurate diagnosis, it allows early diagnosis of diseases like 
cancer [10].

308  Multimodal Data Fusion for Bioinformatics AI
14.2.2	
Predictive Analytics and Personalized Medicine
AI systems, using patients’ data, examine the probability of disease devel­
opment. This creates a push to personalized medicine by allowing people 
to be proactive and adopt customized therapies to prevent, manage or heal 
illnesses [11].
14.2.3	
Natural Language Processing (NLP) for Clinical 
Documentation
NLP is a tool that can help extract key information from clinical notes and 
other unstructured textual data that may be present in electronic health 
records. These are made more practical with the use of this data.
14.2.4	
Virtual Health Assistants and Chatbots
The AI-enabled chatbots and virtual assistants can respond to queries, pro­
vide information, and in some cases help in monitoring a chronic illness 
[12]. This results in patient engagement, successful monitoring and super­
vision plans.
14.2.5	
Drug Discovery and Development
AI accelerates the drug discovery process by evaluating the effectiveness 
of possible therapeutics through the analysis involving massive data sets. 
This reduces the resources and time necessary in bringing the new drugs 
to the market.
14.2.6	
Operational Efficiency and Resource Management
AI spares clinics and hospitals from several non-essential operations by 
enhancing overall efficiency, putting resources to optimal use, and freeing 
humans from boring jobs. Thus it involves patient flow management, sup­
ply chain management and staff scheduling.
14.2.7	
Remote Patient Monitoring
AI can be applied to devices with IoT and monitored patients with chronic 
illnesses remotely whenever necessary. This will enable healthcare provid­
ers to receive warning signals earlier in time and, hopefully, a visit to the 
hospital will be avoided [13].

Emerging Technologies in IoM: AI, Blockchain and Beyond  309
14.2.8	
Fraud Detection and Security
AI is an important feature of security in healthcare data as it is able to 
detect and prevent potential security breaches. It is also possible to detect 
coupon or insurance billing related fraud.
14.2.9	
Ethical Considerations and Bias Mitigation
AI implementation should not forget about ethical issues. Fairness, open­
ness, and the decrease of AI models prejudice towards patient outcomes 
and care are the main goals, according to [14].
14.2.10	
Regulatory Compliance
The rules and regulations must be followed by AI systems in the field of 
healthcare as well. To keep the patient data private and secure legislation 
such as HIPAA needs to be fully complied with.
Even though the use of AI in healthcare carries a huge potential, there 
are some drawbacks, too, for example, data protection, complicated regu­
lations and the need for professional interdisciplinary collaboration, which 
must be taken into consideration. It is important to draw a balance between 
creativity and the moralities involved in the employment of artificial intel­
ligence in healthcare improvement.
14.3	 Blockchain in the Medical Landscape
This technology, originally designed as the basis for the inception of cryp­
tocurrencies like Bitcoin, has found application in various areas of the 
medical field [15]. Blockchain is being applied in the medical profession 
to overcome problems with security of data, interoperability, transparency, 
and trust [6].
14.3.1	
Data Security and Integrity
The ability of blockchain to form a tamper-resistant and transparent book 
is one of the most important features of it. It achieves this objective of pre­
venting data breaches and unauthorised access by making sure that medi­
cal records are impossible to hack in the healthcare industry.

310  Multimodal Data Fusion for Bioinformatics AI
14.3.2	
Interoperability
Blockchain facilitates building a common and interoperable platform of 
patient records. This would allow the problems posed by incompatible 
EHR systems to be solved, thus enabling the automatic exchange of patient 
data amongst healthcare service providers.
14.3.3	
Patient Empowerment
Nowadays, patients control and own their health data by blockchains. The 
patients can do this by allowing specific practitioners and researchers to 
have access to certain parts of their data [17].
14.3.4	
Supply Chain Management
Blockchain makes the pharmaceutical supply chain transparent and trace­
able. Through the production, distribution, and intake monitoring, it 
reduces the chance of counterfeit medicines and enhances the safety of 
patients.
14.3.5	
Clinical Trials and Research
Blockchain, by making the study findings transparent and immutable, 
helps to increase the validity of clinical trial data. It can also foster collab­
oration among researchers and increase others’ trust in research results.
14.3.6	
Smart Contracts
Healthcare operations can be automated through smart contracts, which 
are self-executing agreements where the terms of the agreement are coded 
directly into the code. It is composed of billing, adhering to the treatment 
plans, and processing the insurance claims.
14.3.7	
Identity Management
Blockchain helps identity management by providing a distributed, secure 
method of keeping patient IDs. In healthcare, it can help ensure proper 
patient identification and reduce the case of identity theft.

Emerging Technologies in IoM: AI, Blockchain and Beyond  311
14.3.8	
Credentialing and Certification
Blockchain technology can be used to develop a distributed and immutable 
system for professional credentials [18]. This ensures that healthcare work­
ers have valid and updated certifications at all times.
14.3.9	
Data Sharing and Consent
Through blockchain technology, patients can either allow or deny access to 
specific information pieces, thus providing more precise control over their 
health data. The latter encourages patient trust and complies with privacy 
rules.
14.3.10	
Cybersecurity
Better cybersecurity is the outcome of blockchain because of its decen­
tralized structure [19]. The likelihood of a single point of failure or a sig­
nificant data breach is minimized since the data is not stored in a central 
repository.
Despite any potential benefits, there are still barriers that need to be 
addressed for a wide popularity of blockchain in medicine. Such challenges 
comprise scalability issue, regulatory uncertainty, and the need to have 
standardization across the industry. Blockchain is capable of transforming 
the security, interoperability and transparency of data within healthcare by 
evolving the technology and adjusting the regulatory environments.
14.4	 Benefits of Using Technologies in IoM
There are numerous advantages of the integration of technology in the 
Internet of Medicine (IoM) that transform patient outcomes, medical 
research and health care delivery in different aspects [20]. Figure 14.2 
explains the various advantages associated with the implementation of the 
latest technology in the IoM.
14.4.1	
Remote Monitoring and Telemedicine
Technological advances that allow the monitoring of patients’ vital signs 
and health parameters in real-time are facilitating fast response time and 
omnipresent care by healthcare professionals. Due to telemedicine, remote 

312  Multimodal Data Fusion for Bioinformatics AI
consultations are fostered. Consequently, healthcare services accessibility 
is enhanced, including among the poor or remote areas [21].
14.4.2	
Improved Diagnostics and Treatment
Big data can be processed through the process of AI to detect anomalies, 
forecast the disease path and ensure customized drug recommendations. 
This is likely to lead to more accurate, faster, and effective diagnosis, better 
treatment decisions and in general better results for patients.
14.4.3	
Genomic Medicine and Data Analytics
These are just some examples of the technologies like data analytics and 
genomics which give patients more individualized treatment. Healthcare 
providers are able to enhance the effectiveness of treatment by tailoring 
medicines to suit individual genetics, as they can examine an individual’s 
genetic makeup, as well as the history and data of their particular health.

14.4.4	
Automation and Robotics
In surgical robotics and the automation of operations which are monoto­
nous, productivity can rise, mistake rates can fall to zero and the period of 
recovery may be shortened. The cost may therefore be declining and the 
healthcare processes more effective.
14.4.5	
Wearables and IoT Devices
Patients can get involved in their health management by the use of wear­
ables and Internet of Things (IoT) devices as they can transform their life­
style and get health updates in real time [22]. Such a strategy enhances a 
proactive approach to well-being and one’s feeling of empowerment.
14.4.6	
Virtual Reality (VR) and Augmented Reality (AR)
In conjunction with virtual reality and augmented reality technology, inter­
active simulations can teach medical personnel how to execute compli­
cated tasks without the fear of failure. Furthermore, they also introduce the 
latest research procedures such as the visualization of complex structures.
14.4.7	
Telehealth and Mobile Health (mHealth)
By using mobile apps and telehealth services, patients would be able to 
get information regarding their health and consult the physician remotely. 
Remote areas and the elderly particularly benefit from telemedicine which 
is available through the internet.
14.4.8	
Blockchain for Healthcare Management
The supply chain management, insurance claim processing, and billing are 
the main administrative tasks which the blockchain can instantly expe­
dite. With the effiency and transparancy offered by blockchain technology, 
these risks are kept at bay as well as administrative costs [23].
14.4.9	
Data Analytics and AI in Research
The route to discovering and developing new drugs can be shortened due 
to the use of modern analytics together with AI algorithms to analyze vast 
data sets. Researchers will be able to pinpoint a wide range of possible 

314  Multimodal Data Fusion for Bioinformatics AI
medication candidates faster by using data-driven knowledge and predic­
tive modelling.
14.4.10	
Blockchain and Encryption
Blockchain, in its turn, reduces the possibility of a data breach by ensuring 
the safety and integrity of a patient’s data. Encryption technologies that 
ensure the protection of sensitive data beyond reasonable doubt are also 
deployed, a measure that improves patient confidentiality and compliance 
with data privacy regulations.
Such inclusion not only improves the level of healthcare but also makes 
it accessible, accurate, and patient-oriented. Yet beyond the care of a spe­
cific patient, these benefits include the research that leads to better med­
icine, and the education that guides more efficient delivery of healthcare 
services.
14.5	 Integration of Cutting-Edge Technologies
Blockchain reduces the opportunity of a data breach through verifica­
tion of data safety and integrity. Sensitive data is additionally guarded by 
encryption tools, which in the first place provide patients with confidence 
in privacy and data security [24].
Technology integrations have become essential aspects of healthcare 
systems, increasing their accessibility, effectiveness and patient-centerness. 
However, care for one patient is not the only benefit; research, education, 
access, and the general streamlining of delivery systems will be improved 
too [25]. Through this strategic integration processes, performance and 
capabilities are highlighted because of the combining and inclusion of the 
latest and most advanced technology in the entirety of the system, process, 
or application. This thinking includes by all means the use of the latest inno­
vations to produce a bigger impact that surpasses the potential of any indi­
vidual technology. By establishing smooth interactions and interoperability 
across various cutting-edge technologies, the integration process seeks to 
promote an effective and comprehensive approach to problem-solving or 
optimisation in a variety of disciplines [26]. Emerging technologies like 
artificial intelligence (AI), the Internet of things (IoT), blockchain [27–29], 
augmented reality (AR), virtual reality (VR), and other cutting-edge solu­
tions are frequently essential elements of this integration.
The goal is to utilize the synergies of these technologies to stimulate 
innovation, enhance the efficiency of procedures, and achieve better 

Emerging Technologies in IoM: AI, Blockchain and Beyond  315
performance that surpasses all predecessors in various industries, includ­
ing industry, health, and finance [30]. Thorough analysis, team member 
collaboration, and flexibility should be a priority so that these disruptive 
innovations can work harmoniously leading to this great revolution.
14.6	 Beyond AI and Blockchain: Exploring 
Additional Technologies
It is an essential study of the electronic systems that lie beyond the areas of 
blockchain and artificial intelligence. This concept underlines the existence 
and need to innovate in discovering unique ways of dealing with issues and 
thus improving the sectors and fields [31]. This section presents a thorough 
investigation.
14.6.1	
Internet of Things (IoT)
Data for the healthcare processes is gathered and made available to health 
systems through IoT devices. Examples of this group of machines are 
smart watches and smart medical equipment. This data set includes vital 
signs, such as heart rate, medication adherence and lifestyle measures. IoT 
is used to create smart care, preventive care is made real, and there is real-
time monitoring when IoT is integrated with IoM.
14.6.2	
3D Printing in Medicine
The ability to create customised implants, tissues, and even medical 
devices with the help of 3D printing technology is also feasible. Thus, it 
is now possible to design treatments which would suit each individual 
patient, improving favourable surgical outcomes and reducing probability 
of rejection.
14.6.3	
Augmented Reality (AR) and Virtual Reality (VR)
Immersion of patients into educational categories, surgical planning and 
medical training can be enhanced with the help of AR and VR technol­
ogies. While VR serves the therapeutic interventions and mental health 
treatments, AR permits the surgeons to visualize the anatomy of their 
patients as they operate.

316  Multimodal Data Fusion for Bioinformatics AI
14.6.4	
Genomic Medicine
By using the genetic analysis, in genomic medicine each patient’s medical 
care can be tailored accordingly. What is remarkable is that developments 
in genetics allow diseases to be treated based on genetic predisposition, 
which as a result helps provide more personalised and targeted treatment 
plans.
14.6.5	
Robotics in Surgery
Surgical robots perform minimally invasive procedures in accordance with 
the experienced surgeons’ commands. Through higher precision, fewer 
invasive procedures, and even remote surgery, these robots essentially 
increase the accessibility to specialized cures.
14.6.6	
Natural Language Processing (NLP) and Voice 
Assistants
With NLP, computers become able to understand and operate on human 
language. Voice assistants and NLP (natural language processing) can pro­
vide more accessible and user-friendly IoMT (Internet of Medical Things) 
by providing natural-language interaction between patients and the health­
care system.
14.6.7	
Edge Computing
This means that the processing of data happens at the point of generation, 
thus reducing latency and improving medical data analysis in real time 
[32, 33]. This is one of the key aspects regarding the Internet of Medical 
Things applications that necessitate instantaneous decision making, like 
emergency response systems and remote patient monitoring.
These emergent technologies have the potential to integrate the Internet 
of Medicine and create a more interconnected, proficient, and patient-­
oriented healthcare system in return. These innovations can be expected 
to improve diagnostics accuracy, contribute to enhanced treatment out­
comes, increase accessibility to healthcare services, and ultimately change 
how health-care is delivered and experienced.

Emerging Technologies in IoM: AI, Blockchain and Beyond  317
14.7	 Ethical Considerations in Implementing 
Emerging Technologies
Innovations in emerging technologies have their own set of ethical con­
cerns that organizations and individuals are required to carefully consider. 
key ethical considerations in implementing emerging technologies are 
considered below.
14.7.1	
Privacy Concerns
Emerging technologies frequently include data collection, retention, and 
analysis on an extreme scale. Adherence to privacy rules, transparent data 
practices and having proper user consent for data collection are mandatory 
for ethical implementation.
14.7.2	
Data Security
There is a need to safeguard the data of a sensitive nature as the busi­
ness world is becoming more dependent on data-driven technology. 
Implementors must bear in mind the various cyber risks, unauthorised 
accesses, and data breaches [34] in order to reduce them.
14.7.3	
Bias and Fairness
The algorithms and models of evolving technologies may take unintended 
decisions that expose present biases from previous data. In AI apps, ethical 
issues arise, and we have to make a constant effort to eradicate the bias of 
the AI in order to enjoy impartial and equal outcomes.
14.7.4	
Transparency and Explainability
The mechanisms by which emerging technologies come into action should 
be disclosed to users and all stakeholders. These implementers, necessar­
ily, have to render explainability a top priority, so that a user may have 
the capability to understand the reasoning and decision-making process of 
these technologies.

318  Multimodal Data Fusion for Bioinformatics AI
14.7.5	
Informed Consent
Those who use innovative AI applications must be committed to gaining 
the informed consent of all people who are affected by this and other devel­
oping technologies [16]. With this type of approach, an individual can real­
ize what impact technologies may have on their life activities and ways in 
which data is used.
14.7.6	
Accessibility
It is, however, significant that universal access to emerging technologies is 
guaranteed, no matter the physical constraint, socioeconomic background 
or other variables, for their utilization to be ethical. This means designing 
technology, which in turn doesn’t compound on the pre-existing inequality.
14.7.7	
Job Displacement and Employment Impact
AI and automation involve the risk of job losses at the workplace. Disclosure 
of the effects on employment, retraining of those affected, and a sensible 
adoption of new technology are all ethical issues.
14.7.8	
Environmental Impact
With time, there is an increase in concern about sustainability. Technologies’ 
environmental impact should be evaluated by people who implement the 
technologies. This would include factors such as the amount of waste gen­
erated, the resources consumed, and the energy consumed.
14.7.9	
Dual-Use Concerns
Technologies designed for one goal can have secondary negative effects 
unintendedly and dangerously. Ethical issues reflect the assessment of pos­
sible dual-application bias effects and regulations to avoid misuse.
14.7.10	
Accountability and Liability
Liability and accountability have to be pinned down. The assignment of 
responsibility of those responsible and the strategies to deal with and rectify 
these issues, which may arise as a result of the emergence of new technolo­
gies, causing unintended consequences or harm, is of utmost importance.

Emerging Technologies in IoM: AI, Blockchain and Beyond  319
14.7.11	
Regulatory Compliance
The application of ethics complies with the most recent laws and regu­
lations. All implementers must make sure that they follow relevant legal 
regulations as well as ethical demands to guarantee their technologies have 
these parameters fulfilled.
14.7.12	
Cultural Sensitivity
Technologies are somehow biased to certain cultures and viewpoints. This 
entails paying attention to the cultural environment in which technologies 
are used and ensuring that they are not turned into stereotypes of cultural 
norms.
14.7.13	
Long-Term Impacts
Medium and long-term impact of technology on people, the economy 
and society as well as an ethical dimension are of equal importance. The 
assessor should determine the most probable outcomes and be visionary 
enough to tackle them in the long run.
The approach has to be not only active and multidisciplinary includ­
ing engineers, ethicists, legislators and other stakeholders but also it has to 
be coordinated. With the use of novel technologies being able to promote 
sustainable innovation and to generate a positive social impact becomes 
possible, as long as ethics is given high priority.
14.8	 Conclusion
To summarise, this part of the chapter outlines the extent to which the 
advanced technologies, among others, Artificial Intelligence (AI), block­
chain, and other cutting-edge inventions have on the Internet of (IoM) 
medicine. It showcases the many ways these technologies can be integrated 
into the healthcare system, the benefits they bring, as well as the challenges 
that these technologies may face. This information, therefore, points out 
that AI is a huge game changer in the healthcare industry, where it helps 
in administration of healthcare, designing of personalised treatments, 
and improving diagnosis efforts. Furthermore, it examines blockchain’s 
revolutionary potential in establishing decentralised healthcare systems 
and ensuring data security and openness. Studying other blockchain and 

320  Multimodal Data Fusion for Bioinformatics AI
artificial intelligence technologies beyond the leading edge brings the need 
for partnership and dilemmas to the light.
This chapter emerges as an invaluable aid during the renovation of the 
global healthcare system. It is a repository of invaluable information about 
the way healthcare tech is evolving and the parallel implications of it after 
a certain period of adoption.

Natural Language Processing 
in Biomedical Literature
Molina Mukherjee1, Prachi Punia2, Adil Husain Rather1 and Hardik Dhiman3*
1Department of CSE, UIE, Chandigarh University, Mohali, Punjab, India

Abstract
In this research we have provided an overview of the use of Natural Language 
Processing (NLP) in analysing biomedical literature. We begin with describing 
what biomedical literature is and in what ways it is useful, then we move towards 
understanding the need of NLP in biomedical literature. To understand the con-
text of NLP in biomedical text better, a brief note on the origin of this technology 
is covered here. Additionally, the fundamentals of NLP and the phases through 
which a piece of text goes through in order to draw useful insights from it, is briefly 
described. To understand the kind of data we’ll be analysing better we’ve discussed 
the sources of diversity in biomedical literature. Further we have covered the con-
flicts and disagreements that must be taken into account to make improvements 
in the methodologies being used. This chapter also discusses the emerging trends 
and patterns in the field of NLP in biomedical literature and its useful applications. 
Overall, the chapter summarizes the usefulness, drawbacks and potential of using 
natural language processing to analyse biomedical text.
Keywords:  Natural language processing, biomedical literature, healthcare, 
research, analysis

324  Multimodal Data Fusion for Bioinformatics AI
15.1	 Introduction
Biomedical literature is a collective noun used to refer to data from various 
research publications, clinical data gathered from different fields like anat-
omy, genetics, physiology, epidemiology, etc. It acts as a source of reference 
for people working in the healthcare industry. These kinds of manuscripts 
contain scientific findings, current advancements in the field of medicine 
and epidemiology trends and helps researchers and medical professionals 
to find information related to treatments, drug discovery and many such 
other things. Reviewing the existing literature is a major aspect in identi-
fication of gaps in current knowledge which can aid researchers in build-
ing hypotheses and design experiments for new discoveries (Burugadda, 
Pawar, Kumar, & Bhati, 2023). Biomedical literature is typically published 
in journals that are peer-reviewed; consequently, the published work has 
to undergo diligent evaluation procedures by experts of the field prior to 
its release. This ensures reliability and quality of the information presented 
in these publications. There are numerous academic libraries, online data-
bases and scholarly publishing platforms through which these literature 
works can be accessed. The primary source of freely accessible textual 
data information in biomedicine and molecular biology is MEDLINE. It 
encompasses over 12,000,000 abstracts maintained by National Library of 
Medicine (NLM), accessible through the PubMed suite (Krallinger et al., 
2005). Protein information is majorly accessed through another database 
like SwissProt.
Retrieval and interpretation of biomedical literature poses significant 
hurdles for students, professionals or the general public. The boundless 
amount of literature that is already available can lead to information over-
load, resulting in difficulties in identification of relevant articles for any 
specific topic of interest or clinical question. The literature and the data 
keeps on increasing at a tremendous rate with each passing minute. It can 
be found in many different formats like reviews, articles, preprints, etc., 
and imposes challenges in prioritizing and navigating through the infor-
mation. Technical terminology that is used in this literature is most of the 
time not intelligible for individuals who do not have a strong background 
in the field. To address many such challenges we need to devise efficient 
search strategies or employ advanced search tools and techniques that will 
make comprehension of the biomedical literature trouble-free.
Natural language processing (NLP) can significantly aid in compre-
hending biomedical literature by utilization of computational methods 
for extraction, analysis and interpretation of relevant information from 

Natural Language Processing in Biomedical Literature  325
the vast literature repository. NLP algorithms are employed to mine text 
data from biomedical literature available in the databases or the clinical 
notes. Using these algorithms, desired information can be extracted such 
as gene sequences, protein interactions, drug compounds, associations and 
treatments of diseases from unstructured data, thus aiding researchers in 
getting indispensable insights and knowledge.
One of the recently advancing branches of biomedical is proteomics. 
It involves protein analysis for understanding their roles in various bio-
logical pathways, cellular processes and disease mechanisms, and study 
of post translational modifications and investigation of protein-protein 
interactions (McArdle and Menikou, 2021). NLP algorithms are useful 
to mine text data related to proteins and aid in understanding of protein 
biology and its implications in health, treatments and drug discoveries.. 
They can extract critical information including amino acid sequences, dif-
ferent domains of proteins, structural motifs, and functional annotations 
(Krallinger et al., 2005). By analysing this information, researchers can get 
a better understanding of structural characteristics of proteins, their fold-
ing patterns, and how they would interact with another protein or a target 
molecule or receptor in the biological system. The search of novel drug tar-
gets majorly relies on computational methods that aids in identifying the 
most suitable protein based on its properties. Which can then be followed 
up by laboratory procedures for confirmations.
The process of understanding the context of words or phrases within 
a text data and analysing relation between entities such as genes, pro-
teins, drugs, symptoms and many other biomedical concepts is termed 
as semantic analysis and entity recognition (Krallinger et al., 2005). NLP 
tools can aid in identification and categorization of the entities and struc-
turing the information available. The information extracted is also used in 
recognizing relationships between the entities which can in turn be used 
for constructing knowledge graphs. This is particularly of assistance in 
understanding molecular pathways and disease mechanisms.
Lengthy biomedical texts can be easily summarized with the help of NLP 
tools that retrieve key information and offer concise overviews of research 
finding, treatment outcomes or clinical studies. This makes the informa-
tion understandable as well as making the search convenient. Hence, nav-
igation through large volumes of text can be performed more efficiently 
and in less time.
Another powerful tool of molecular biology is microarray. It is used 
to measure the expression levels of thousands of genes simultaneously 
(Lysov et al., 2017). It enables scientists to study how the genes are turned 
on or off in different conditions and provide insight into gene regulations, 

326  Multimodal Data Fusion for Bioinformatics AI
identifying biomarkers of different diseases, and discovery of potential 
drug targets. Microarrays provide an extensive pool of data points which 
is too complex for human interpretation and analysis and hence requires 
computational methods or NLP for proper understanding and compre-
hension (Burugadda, Jadhav, Vyas, & Duggar, 2023).
NLP can be used for the retrieval of gene sequences and functions 
which can further be subjected to sequence alignment tools to figure out 
evolutionary relationships between numerous existing species as well as 
predicting sequences of the extinct species that no longer exist. Many dif-
ferent algorithms like ClustalW, MUSCLE and MAFFT are being used for 
DNA alignment or protein sequencing.
In conclusion, applying NLP in different biomedical fields improves 
knowledge discovery, retrieval of necessary information and interpreta-
tion of complex data, which in turn enhances clinical decision making and 
assists in reaching new milestones in biomedical research and healthcare 
practices worldwide.
15.2	 History
The use of Natural Language Processing (NLP) in biomedical literature is a 
proof of the significance of computational linguistics and its usefulness in 
biomedical research and treatment. Various tools, softwares and methods 
have been invented and deployed to extract useful insights from biomed-
ical texts.
NLP was first used in the field of biomedicine to analyse text and per-
form computer-assisted retrieval. In the 1960s, the idea of information 
retrieval systems was first talked of, when researchers looked at automatic 
indexing and searching of biomedical texts. The creation of MEDLINE 
database by National Library of Medicine (NLM) in 1964 was considered a 
revolutionary achievement, which was created with the intention of mak-
ing indexing and abstraction of biological literature easy (Humphreys & 
Lindberg, 1993).
NLP techniques and algorithms have seen enormous growth during the 
1970s and 1980s. Tasks like named entity recognition, syntactic parsing 
and categorization were performed using NLP during these years. NLP 
applications saw significant development with invention of text mining 
systems like LEMIMI and Protee.
The 1990s proved to be a time of great significance for the field of sci-
ence with the growth of NLP and bioinformatics. With the introduction of 
internet the amount and availability of biomedical literature significantly 

Natural Language Processing in Biomedical Literature  327
increased. Many scientists started using Natural Language Processing 
techniques for knowledge discovery, information extraction from text and 
biomedical text summarization. Scientists collaborated a lot during this 
time with the introduction of initiatives like Protein Information Resource 
(PIR) and BioCreative project (Cohen, 2005).
During the last few years the combination of NLP with other interdisci-
plinary fields such as genomics, proteomics and clinical informatics has led 
to development of new applications and methodologies. These include the 
Clinical Text Analysis and Knowledge Extraction System (cTAKES) and 
OpenAI Bio platform, which have helped in demonstrating the usefulness 
of NLP in biomedical text normalization, drug discovery and phenotypic 
extraction.
Natural language processing (NLP) is of paramount importance in cases 
where scientists or researchers want to find hidden patterns and draw use-
ful insights from biomedical texts. Apart from the benefits there are also 
some challenges in the use of NLP in biomedical literature like the need 
for annotated datasets, ontologies and domain specific language models. 
Interdisciplinary collaborations help to create robust NLP solutions specif-
ically to analyse biomedical literature (Gupta and Kumar, 2018)
Over the years NLP has changed the way in which information is 
extracted from biomedical text. Interdisciplinary cooperation and the use 
of modern NLP techniques has allowed scholars to draw useful insights 
from the vast amounts of biomedical textual data, resulting in advance-
ment in healthcare delivery, research and medicine discovery (Kumar, 
Kumar, Dutt, Dubey, & Narang, 2023).
15.3	 Theoretical Foundation: Natural Language 
Processing in Scientific Writing
15.3.1	
Natural Language Processing (NLP) Overview
Natural Language Processing is a interdisciplinary and one of the various 
subfields of Artificial Intelligence which in simple words enables com-
puters to understand and generate human-like language using various 
algorithms.
15.3.2	
Key Concepts in NLP
•	 Tokenization: It refers to dividing words into units or parts

328  Multimodal Data Fusion for Bioinformatics AI
•	 Part-of-Speech (POS) Tagging: Labelling of words accord-
ing to their grammatical context
•	 Named Entity Recognition (NER): It refers to the extraction 
of information from unstructured literature and classifying 
it into different groups which can be proteins, patient cate-
gories, genes, etc.
•	 Syntactic parsing: Analysis of the structure of a sentence to 
understand the relation between the words and the way they 
are arranged.
•	 Semantic analysis: Interpreting the actual meaning of words 
by understanding the context of the sentence in which they 
appear.
15.3.3	
Biomedical Text Analysis Using Natural Language 
Processing (NLP)
The steps below cover the journey of data from raw sources to processed 
output, that has been generated by going through various phases of natural 
language processing. Figure 15.1 visually represents this journey of trans-
formation of raw data to insightful data using NLP.
15.3.3.1	
Beginning Point: Unprocessed Biomedical Text 
Information
Data is collected from various sources such as research papers, clinical 
notes and electronic health records. This data is given as input to the next 
phase.
15.3.3.2	
Data Preprocessing
•	 Tokenization: The data collected is divided into parts or 
tokens (Manning et al., 2009).
•	 Stopword Removal: To concentrate on the important mate-
rial, frequently used words that don’t have much meaning—
like “the,” “and,” and “of”—are eliminated.
•	 Lemmatization/Stemming: To minimise inflectional forms 
and variants, words are reduced to their base or root form.

Natural Language Processing in Biomedical Literature  329
15.3.3.3	
Feature Extraction
•	 Named Entity Recognition (NER): NER performs the 
extraction of information from unstructured text and classi-
fication of it into different groups. In this case the entities can 
be proteins, patient categories, genes, etc. (Wei et al., 2013).
•	 Part-of-Speech (POS) Tagging: The words in the text are 
divided into adjectives, verbs, and nouns and labelled.
•	 Dependency Parsing: To understand the connection 
between the words present in the text, the syntactic struc-
ture of the sentences is examined.

15.3.3.4	
Interpretation and Analysis
•	 Information Extraction: Useful insights such as relation-
ships, biological concepts, facts, discoveries are extracted 
from the text.
•	 Entity Relationship Extraction: This step interprets the 
associations between the words hidden within the text.
•	 Text Summarization: In order to enhance readability 
and to make it easy to understand the insights, the data is 
summarized.
15.3.3.5	
Specific Tasks for Applications
•	 Exploiting Literature: The extracted information is used to 
perform trend analysis, clustering and knowledge discovery 
from biomedical text (Cohen, 2005).
•	 Clinical Decision Support System: The information 
obtained from medical texts is utilized to assist healthcare 
professionals in identifying diseases, suggesting treatments, 
and recognizing events (Chapman et al., 2011).
•	 Knowledge Discovery: By discovering new correlations, 
trends, and patterns, insights from text analysis help to 
advance biomedical research and healthcare delivery.
15.3.3.6	
Final Product: Knowledge and Insights from Biomedical 
Text Data
Using NLP for biomedical texts, useful information can be extracted from 
the text.
This information can be used in biomedical research and doctors can 
use this information in many applications to improve the medical industry 
(Punugoti, Dutt, Anand, & Bhati, 2023).
15.4	 Sources of Diversity in Biomedical Literature’s 
Natural Language Processing
The efficiency of Natural Language Processing (NLP) algorithms are sub-
jected to a variety of sources of variation found in biomedical literature. In 
order to understand how these factors affect the NLP algorithms we’ll now 
see the sources of variation.

Natural Language Processing in Biomedical Literature  331
15.4.1	
Complexity of Linguistics
Linguistic complexity is one of the important sources of variance. The data 
is collected from various sources such as clinical notes, patient records, 
research articles. Each type of text has unique linguistic characteristic, 
writing style and terminology. Often NLP algorithms face difficulties in 
properly analysing text due to use of jargon, acronyms, etc. (Cohen and 
Hunter, 2008).
15.4.2	
Heterogeneity of Data
As mentioned above, data in biomedical literature is collected from a vari-
ety of sources and is heterogeneous in nature. Each source holds a different 
characteristic, the analysis of multimodal data types, such as text, images 
and structured data (Zargar et al., 2023).
15.4.3	
Variability in Annotation
Annotation guidelines, expert opinions and subjective interpretation are 
few of the ways in which variability is introduced in the annotation process 
of biomedical text for NER and relation extraction. The dependability of 
annotated datasets used in the NLP model is based on annotator consis-
tency and agreement. This can certainly affect the performance of Natural 
Language Processing algorithms (Khaki et al., 2020).
15.4.4	
Domain Specificity
Biomedical literature covers data from a wide range of fields like clinical 
medicine, pharmacology, and genetics. Each of these fields has a differ-
ent set of vocabulary needs, concepts associated and methods to represent 
information (Kumar, Kumar, Dutt, & Soni, 2021). NLP algorithms that are 
trained on data containing normal or what we call vocabulary that can 
be understood by normal people may face difficulties in dealing with the 
jargon found in medical writings. To handle these domain-specific diffi-
culties, domain-specific strategies and specialised resources are needed 
(Alsentzer et al., 2019).
These sources of variance hinder the development of robust natural lan-
guage processing algorithms that are used to analyse biomedical text. With 
the interdisciplinary cooperation, domain specific resources, standardised 
annotation processes and machine learning algorithms these issues can be 
handled.

332  Multimodal Data Fusion for Bioinformatics AI
15.5	 Disagreement and Conflict
The use of Natural Language Processing in biomedical literature is a vast 
area of discussion and hence there are many conflicts and disagreements 
associated with it. Methodological discrepancies, data heterogeneity, and 
interpretation difficulties are a few of them.
It is often difficult to choose the perfect NLP technique and algorithm. 
Suppose if different teams are working on item recognition, relation 
extraction and text classification then there are chances that there will be 
differences in how different research groups interpret the result (Smith and 
Ceusters, 2010) The lack of standardization makes it difficult to conduct 
comparative analysis, which acts as a reason for disagreement between 
researchers (Ehrmann et al., 2024).
NLP models that are trained on general datasets may find it difficult 
to interpret literature with medical jargon, linguistics patterns and com-
plex writing style (Manning et al., 2009). This can lead to lack of gener-
alization and poor performance across various biomedical domains due 
to differences in the performance of NLP systems (CheshmehSohrabi and 
Mashhadi, 2023).
Usually NLP algorithms are considered highly accurate in extracting 
information from raw data but at times they may give rise to conflict in 
the way in which they are interpreted by NLP, since interpretation needs 
domain knowledge. Diverse interpretations and conclusions may lead to 
biases, subjective interpretation and disparities (Friedman and Hripcsak, 
1999).
Hence, it can be concluded that data heterogeneity, diverse method-
ologies, and interpretation difficulties may cause disagreement about the 
efficiency of NLP to analyse biomedical texts. In order to overcome these 
issues interdisciplinary cooperation, transparent feedback system and 
robust testing methods should be employed.
15.6	 Natural Language Processing Trends  
and Patterns in Biomedical Literature
Nowadays the use of NLP in mining biomedical text has significantly 
increased. This has opened ways to many scientific discoveries, healthcare 
services and research fields. Let’s take a look at the emerging trends and 
patterns.

Natural Language Processing in Biomedical Literature  333
15.6.1	
Graphs of Biomedical Knowledge
Biomedical knowledge graphs give a pictorial representation of biological 
concepts, entities, the interaction between them and the facts linked to it. 
The data represented in knowledge graphs in this case is collected from 
electronic health records (EHRs), ontologies, literature databases, etc. 
Graph-based representation of entities makes semantic search easy and 
fast and therefore it is quite handy in NLP-based systems. Figure 15.2 is an 
example of a biomedical knowledge graph that represents how genes and 
gene products are referenced by the gene node.
15.6.2	
In-Depth Learning Frameworks
Deep Learning is widely used in the field of biomedical natural language 
processing. Transformer based models like BERT (Bidirectional Encoder 
Representations from Transformers) and GPT (Generative Pre-trained 
Transformer) are two of the extensively used models and are pretrained on a 
large quantity of medical text. These models can perform relation extraction, 
entity recognition and document categorization. The efficiency of such mod-
els can be significantly increased by fine-tuning them using domain-specific 
data (Beltagy et al., 2019; Swarna, Kumar, Dixit, & Sairam, 2021).

334  Multimodal Data Fusion for Bioinformatics AI
15.6.3	
Multimodal Analysis of Biomedical Data
The nature of data collected from biomedical sources is heterogeneous 
in nature. Molecular sequences, clinical notes and medical photographs 
are some instances. This kind of data can be effectively analysed using 
NLP, computer vision and bioinformatics methods (Sasubilli, Kumar, & 
Dutt, 2020). This approach helps to interpret complex data and facilitate 
research, discovery and diagnosis.
15.6.4	
Regulatory and Ethical Aspects
Privacy is one of the major concerns in this area, hence proper measures, 
legal frameworks and no-bias NLP algorithms should be taken into con-
sideration. This can be made possible with cooperation between research-
ers, legislators, and industry stakeholders (Rajkomar et al., 2018).
15.6.5	
Results
These emerging trends in the field of biomedical literature have signifi-
cantly contributed to the advancements in biomedical research, medical 
assistance and scientific discovery. Key areas of innovation and investi-
gation in the discipline include multimodal data analysis, ethical consid-
erations, deep learning architectures, and biomedical knowledge graphs. 
Through the utilisation of these patterns, scientists and medical practi-
tioners can get fresh perspectives, quicken breakthroughs, and enhance 
patient results in the field of biomedicine.
15.7	 Natural Language Processing’s Useful 
Applications in Biomedical Literature
Natural language processing and literature mining makes the process of 
text mining extremely easy. By defining some sort of criteria, the keyword 
or fact search in an article can be automated. Professionals from the medi-
cal field can use this technology to find trends, patterns in biomedical text. 
Techniques like sentiment analysis, topic modelling and document cluster-
ing can be used to draw insights from the text (Wei et al., 2013).

Natural Language Processing in Biomedical Literature  335
15.7.1	
Annotated Text in Biomedicine
NLP technology helps to annotate text; it makes the annotation process 
extremely easy. Explanatory notes regarding the relationships, named enti-
ties and medical facts can be added (Zhou et al., 2014).
15.7.2	
Systems for Clinical Decision Support (CDSS)
CDSS systems are powered by NLP. As shown in Figure 15.3. These sys-
tems help medical professionals to draw insights from electronic health 
records (EHRs) and clinical notes. Natural language processing algorithms 
can be used to find and extract the hidden meaning from medical texts, 
which in turn assists medical professionals to find patterns, treatment rec-
ommendations and diagnosis of different diseases.

15.7.3	
Investigating Biomedical Information
Apart from just finding hidden patterns, this technology can help in dis-
covering new information simply by identifying relationships, correlations 
and patterns in the data. This helps to contribute information to the world 
of biomedical science.
15.7.4	
Biomedical Text Synopsis
The volume of medical texts present in the world is humongous, and it’s 
not possible to extract knowledge from all of them manually. Hence, NLP 
tools help to easily summarize the text and allow users to get the essence of 
clinical notes, research papers, etc.
15.7.5	
Information Retrieval and Semantic Search
NLP-based information retrieval tools help in understanding user queries 
and give results accordingly. This helps in fast retrieval of information. 
These systems use ontologies, sophisticated language models, and seman-
tic indexing techniques to extract and deliver precise responses to queries.
15.7.6	
Evaluation of Quality and Bias Identification
It’s extremely important to check the text for biases in order to assess the 
quality and reliability of the literature. NLP tools can be used to analyse the 
methodology, design and reporting quality. Overall it helps in maintaining 
the integrity of the text and helps users to draw conclusions (Li et al., 2023).
By utilising the above methods, medical practitioners, researchers and 
healthcare professionals can effectively utilize biomedical texts to obtain 
useful data which in turn will contribute to the advancements in medical 
science.
15.8	 Future Prospects of NLP in Biomedical 
Literature
Driven by new research areas and rising trends, the field of natural lan-
guage processing (NLP) in biomedical literature has enormous promise 
for revolutionary advances in the future. Future directions in this field aim 
to address obstacles, take use of technology improvements, and explore 

Natural Language Processing in Biomedical Literature  337
fresh applications in order to further boost scientific discovery, healthcare 
delivery, and biomedical research.
The invention and integration of efficient deep earning architectures 
can be used to analyse biomedical text and can open gates to many other 
inventions. Transformer-based models are considered very efficient. Two 
of the popular transformer-based models are GPT (Generative Pre-trained 
Transformer) and BERT (Bidirectional Encoder Representations from 
Transformers). These tools help in smooth analysis of biomedical texts 
(Beltagy et al., 2019).
Data processed by NLP in this field is multimodal in nature; it can 
be images, text or any other structured or unstructured data. NLP users 
can analyse this heterogeneous data by using NLP and computer vision 
together. The tools developed can be used to facilitate medication develop-
ment, predictive analysis, etc. (Li et al., 2023).
Ethical practices are quite important in healthcare industries. Patient 
data privacy, security and vulnerability of algorithms to bias are a few of 
the ethical concerns (Rajkomar et al., 2018).
While developing NLP solutions to analyse biomedical literature, devel-
opers should make sure to take care of ethical considerations, technical 
innovation and interdisciplinary collaboration. The information derived 
from these biomedical texts can be used by researchers to find emerging 
trends, new insights and overcome issues faced in the healthcare industry.
15.9	 Conclusion
With the help of NLP, healthcare professionals and researchers can now 
analyse texts and draw conclusions much faster than traditional methods. 
In this chapter we have summarized the need, origin and fundamentals of 
NLP such as tokenization, part-of-speech tagging and named entity recog-
nition. The use of NLP in biomedical texts has found to be of paramount 
significance in areas like biomedical research healthcare delivery, which is 
possible due to the effectiveness on NLP in information extraction, litera-
ture mining, clinical decision support and semantic search.
Apart from all the benefits, there are certain aspects that act as a hin-
drance, such as domain specificity, data heterogeneity, lingual complex-
ity and variability in annotations. To cope with this issues it is important 
to include domain-specific resource, standardised annotation methods 
and cooperation between professionals from different fields of expertise. 
Taking these steps greatly contributes to the increase in robustness of NLP 
algorithms in the biomedical field.

338  Multimodal Data Fusion for Bioinformatics AI
Technical advancements in bioinformatics have resulted in introduc-
ing deep learning architectures, multimodal data analysis and biomedical 
knowledge graphs. With the help of these methods scientists and research-
ers can get quick breakthroughs and enhance patient results in the field of 
biomedicine.
Natural Language Processing in the biomedical field is a thriving field 
that has the potential to greatly influence the world of AI and medicine. By 
embracing cross-disciplinary collaboration, investing in emerging trends 
and addressing the challenges in this field, healthcare professionals can 
harness the power of NLP to improve biomedical research quality, health-
care delivery and contribute to the betterment of mankind.

Biomedical Research Enrichment Through 
Sentiment Analysis in Patient Feedback: 
A Natural Language Processing Approach
Soumitra Saha1, Umesh Kumar Lilhore2* and Sarita Simaiya2
1Department of Computer Science and Engineering Chandigarh University 
Punjab, India
2Galgotias University, Greater Noida, UP, India
Abstract
This chapter consults the trajectory committed by utilizing patient feedback (PF) 
in the wake of biomedical research through sentimental analysis (SA) in natural 
language processing (NLP). PF has been compared to a gold mine for the health­
care industry, delivering clinical efficacy, preserving quality, and overall insight 
into a disorder. Analyzing these patient responses is vastly more time-consuming 
and more likely to be subjective. However, employing SA can efficiently extract 
beneficial insights from this feedback by automating patients’ positive, negative, or 
neutral sentiments. By systematically investigating thousands or millions of obser­
vations to identify familiar themes, distinct concerns, and patient satisfaction lev­
els, researchers can employ these sentiments to understand disease status, improve 
patient care, and assist in making intelligent decisions by analyzing judgments. 
Miscellaneous traditional methods depend on surveys or structured question­
naires to accumulate PF that fails to deliver the preferred results in terms of sen­
timent. On the other hand, by studying sentiment data from mixed social media 
posts and electronic health records, SA can work with structured and unstruc­
tured data to furnish favorable results, allowing researchers to sweeten the grade 
of biomedical research. Eventually, this chapter discloses worthwhile wisdom to 
enrich biomedical research by employing PF through SA.
Keywords:  Biomedical research, natural language processing, healthcare, 
sentimental analysis, machine learning

16.1	 Introduction
The 21st century has witnessed numerous remarkable technological and 
expansion developments, and human civilization is now beginning to reap 
the benefits of this phenomenal technological breakthrough. This transfor­
mation continues, and the breakthrough will become even more widespread 
as the search for new technologies continues. When anyone speaks about the 
contribution in terms of this success of technology, then the most enormous 
contribution behind this success is the abundance of data. In order to solve 
various problems in different sectors, data are of different types depending 
on the problem. Observing the right problem makes it possible to achieve 
the best results for a particular problem by using appropriate data. The accu­
racy or performance of any classification can be enhanced if the number of 
training data is increased to solve a particular problem [1].
Along with this phenomenal improvement in technology, the amount 
of data worldwide has also increased to a large extent, and various types of 
data are constantly being generated for different problems. When only the 
relevant data can be used from the data collected from various sources, the 
irrelevant and relatively less critical data can be excluded, and the correct 
approach can be adopted to solve the problem. Achieving the desired results 
will therefore become much more manageable. The main reason for exclud­
ing irrelevant data is that it can increase the reaction time of the model, and 
the classification accuracy of a given model will be poor [2]. The use of data 
in any sector, including healthcare, education, transportation, agriculture, 
energy, manufacturing, and telecommunication, is undeniable, and using 
data in appropriate models is the primary tool to achieve the best results. 
Artificial intelligence (AI) is the tool through which the desired results can be 
easily achieved by solving any problem using appropriate data.
AI refers to creating a computer system that can presume like a human, 
make judgments like a human, and assist in achieving desired outcomes 
by solving problems in the best possible way out of existing solutions. The 
primary reason for using AI is to efficiently and accurately work on specific 
problems and find optimal results, which is much more time-consuming 
for a human. AI can be compared to the human brain, like intelligence, 
when considered the gold standard [3]. AI is a specific algorithm or pro­
gram that assists a specific machine by using previous experience and con­
sequently providing desired results by using new inputs to solve the most 
challenging problems that are almost unsolvable for a human being. Figure 
16.1 illustrates the diverse subfields available in AI and the distinct appli­
cations in NLP.

the existing data, play an essential role in making the model effective by 
eliminating unnecessary information, finding the existing patterns in the 
data, contradicting and helping to find interdependent data. They succeed 
in making appropriate decisions by using all these information and details 
[4]. AI is divided into two parts depending on the work variation. One is

that can consider all areas, is used to solve the most formidable problems,

sub-sections that are used in different tasks, and some of the main essential

results in less time and with less power consumption. With the help of NLP,

on relevance [6]. In terms of language, NLP’s principal and only task is 
to try to understand, interpret, and provide good results from the various 
languages ​used by different human groups. Input data can be of various 
types: text, audio, video, and handwritten. To work with these data, NLP 
extensively uses unique techniques such as statistical methods, ML, and 
deep learning (DL) algorithms. These algorithms mainly work on enor­

and accuracy of these algorithms increase to a large extent. In the 21st cen­

essential role by preprocessing data from a dataset containing a particular 
language and later extracting information from that processed data, which 
helps with various applications such as sentimental analysis (SA), machine 
translation, text classification, speech recognition, etc. NLP uses a variety of 
processes for a particular application, depending on the task type, impor­
tance, problem description, and expected results. The neurons in artificial 
neural networks (ANN) are interconnected, with thousands to millions of 
hidden layers in the connections. Hidden layers can perform tasks intelli­
gently and quickly transfer significant results to the next layer. That is why 
DL and ANN are now widely used in all NLP activities, as they are cor­
related, and the advancement of DL is breathtaking in miscellaneous types 
of NLP applications [7]. How well the languages ​used can be processed 
depends on how well and cleanly a particular application can perform its 
functions. The type of algorithm that can be used depends mainly on the 
specific application and the task it will perform. These widely used and 
popular applications are now widely used in education, health, military 
intelligence, industry, and other vital sectors.

among them [8]. Almost all applications involving NLP are now used to
